--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Score is MINIMISED (lower = better)
- Nest = WHITE floor, Source = BLACK floor
- One half of source is BLUE (decreases score by 1 when delivered = GOOD)
- Other half is GREEN (increases score by 1 when delivered = BAD)
- We want robots to: go to the BLUE side, pick up items, return to nest (WHITE)

**Key insight from champion:**
- `--s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 2` = GoAwayColor with color 2 (GREEN)
- Color codes: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
- Going AWAY from GREEN walls means robots tend toward BLUE side! This is clever.
- Score -7.73 is the best so far.

**What we need:**
- Robots should collect BLUE items (go to BLACK floor near BLUE walls)
- Deliver to WHITE floor (nest)
- Avoid GREEN items

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion (GoAwayColor GREEN)
2. Try GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) - directly target blue walls
3. Try 2-state machines: explore/navigate + return to nest
4. Key colors: RED=1 (nest wall), GREEN=2, BLUE=3
5. Floor: BLACK=0 (source), GRAY=1 (middle), WHITE=2 (nest)

**Transition logic:**
- When on BLACK floor (at source) → got an item → go to WHITE (nest)
- When on WHITE floor (at nest) → delivered → go back to source
- GoToColor BLUE (3) to find the right source
- GoAwayColor GREEN (2) to avoid wrong source
- When on WHITE floor with item → delivered

**Machine designs:**

1. **Champion** (exact copy): GoAwayColor GREEN
2. **Exploitation**: GoToColor BLUE (clr=3) - go toward blue walls
3. **2-state**: GoAwayColor GREEN → when WHITE floor, GoToColor BLUE → when BLACK
4. **2-state**: GoToColor BLUE → when WHITE, stop/explore → when Black
5. **3-state foraging**: Explore → find BLACK → GoToColor BLUE → WHITE floor → GoAwayColor RED (go toward nest side by going away from red... wait, GoAwayColor RED would go away from nest)

Actually, RED wall is on the NEST side. So GoToColor RED (1) = go toward nest. GoAwayColor RED = go away from nest (toward source).

Better plan:
- State A: GoToColor BLUE (3) - find blue source
- On WHITE (delivered): stay in A or switch
- On BLACK (got item): GoToColor RED (1) - go to nest

Let me design the 14 machines:

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 4 --clr0 2

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of champion:**
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (3) → when BLACK floor (at source, p=0.9) → go to State 1
- State 1: GoToColor RED (1) → when WHITE floor (at nest, p=0.9) → go back to State 0

This is elegant foraging: go to BLUE side of source, pick up item, go to RED wall (nest side), deliver, repeat. Score -16.44 is excellent.

**Key insight:** The BLUE walls mark the good source side. GoToColor BLUE gets robots there. GoToColor RED gets them back to the nest. The BLACK floor trigger fires when they're at the source (picked up item), WHITE floor trigger fires when at nest (delivered).

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Vary probabilities (0.9 → 0.85, 0.95)
3. Add extra transitions to handle edge cases
4. Try GoAwayColor GREEN instead of GoToColor BLUE
5. Try 3-state variants adding exploration or intermediate states
6. Try different floor probability thresholds

**Key observations from best performers:**
- GoToColor BLUE (3) + GoToColor RED (1) is the winning pattern
- GoAwayColor GREEN (2) also works well (score -11.71 uses it)
- Simple 2-state machines outperform complex ones
- The transition probabilities around 0.8-0.9 work well

**Designs:**

1. Champion (exact)
2. Champion with p=0.95 for both transitions
3. Champion with p=0.85 for both
4. Champion with p=0.9 BLACK + p=0.95 WHITE
5. Champion + extra GRAY transition from state 1 (small prob to go back)
6. 3-state: GoToColor BLUE → (BLACK) → GoToColor RED → (WHITE) → GoToColor BLUE with intermediate exploration
7. 2-state GoAwayColor GREEN → (BLACK) → GoToColor RED → (WHITE)
8. 2-state GoToColor BLUE → (BLACK,p=0.9) + (WHITE,p=0.1) → GoToColor RED → (WHITE)
9. 3-state adding GoAwayColor GREEN as initial state
10. Variant of #2 best performer with adjusted probs
11. 2-state with GoToColor BLUE and GoAwayColor GREEN combo
12. 3-state champion-derived with extra state for exploration

## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**
The champion pattern is crystal clear:
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (3), transition on BLACK floor (p=0.9) → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor RED (1), transition on WHITE floor (p=0.95) → State 0

The top 4 performers are all variants of this same 2-state structure with slightly different probabilities. The sweet spot seems to be p≈0.9 for BLACK trigger and p≈0.95 for WHITE trigger.

**Key observations:**
- Adding extra transitions (camera, neighbor-based) HURTS performance (worst performers)
- 3+ state machines perform worse
- The simple 2-state GoToColor BLUE → GoToColor RED structure is optimal
- p=0.9/0.95 beats p=0.95/0.95 and p=0.85/0.85

**Exploitation:** Fine-tune probabilities around 0.9/0.95 sweet spot
- Try: 0.88, 0.92, 0.90, 0.93, 0.97 for the WHITE trigger
- Try: 0.88, 0.92, 0.95 for the BLACK trigger

**Exploration:** 
- Try GoToColor BLUE with GRAY floor trigger (might catch robots passing through)
- Try adding a very low probability random transition
- Try 3-state with GoAwayColor GREEN as initial scout
- Try GoToColor BLUE → BLACK → GoAwayColor GREEN → WHITE (inverted approach)

The key insight: p=0.9 on BLACK seems robust. p=0.95 on WHITE seems slightly better than 0.9. Let me explore the 0.9-0.99 range for the WHITE transition more carefully.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from champion:**
The new champion has TWO transitions in State 0:
- Transition 0: BLACK floor, p=0.9 → go to State 1 (got item at source, head to nest)
- Transition 1: WHITE floor, p=0.05 → go to State 0 (stay at source if somehow at nest already)

Wait, both transitions go to State 0 (destination index 0 = the OTHER state = State 1). Actually with 2 states, `--n0x0 0` means destination index 0 among others = State 1, and `--n0x1 0` also means State 1. So both transitions go to State 1.

Actually re-reading: with 2 states, leaving state 0, the only other state is state 1, so D=0 always targets state 1. So:
- State 0 (GoToColor BLUE): when BLACK (p=0.9) OR when WHITE (p=0.05) → go to State 1
- State 1 (GoToColor RED): when WHITE (p=0.95) → go back to State 0

The WHITE floor trigger in State 0 (p=0.05) means: if a robot somehow arrives at the nest without an item, it has a small chance to switch to return mode. This prevents robots from getting stuck at the nest when they shouldn't be there.

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Vary the WHITE trigger probability in State 0: try 0.03, 0.04, 0.06, 0.07, 0.08, 0.10
- Vary the BLACK trigger in State 0: try 0.88, 0.92, 0.95
- Vary the WHITE trigger in State 1: try 0.92, 0.97, 1.0
- Try adding a GRAY floor trigger to State 1 (small prob to switch back if lost)
- Try adding a second transition to State 1

**Exploration:**
- Try adding BLACK floor trigger to State 1 (if robot goes back to source accidentally)
- Try 3-state with the champion topology extended
- Try different color combinations

## Reasoning

**Situation assessment:**
The champion (-17.05) has been stable for 2 rounds. We're converging on a 2-state machine with:
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE(3), BLACK→S1(p=0.9), WHITE→S1(p=0.05)
- State 1: GoToColor RED(1), WHITE→S0(p=0.95), BLACK→S0(p=0.05)

Key findings:
- Adding 3rd transitions to either state hurts badly
- p=0.04 for the BLACK trigger in State 1 hurts vs p=0.05
- p=0.88 for the BLACK trigger in State 0 was second best this round (-16.24)
- p=0.97 in State 1 WHITE trigger also decent (-16.14)
- The champion parameters (0.9/0.05 / 0.95/0.05) are very competitive

**Exploitation focus:**
The parameter space around the champion is well-explored. Let me try:
- Varying BLACK p in State 0: 0.86, 0.87, 0.89, 0.91
- Varying WHITE p in State 0: 0.04, 0.05, 0.06 (already done, all similar)
- Varying WHITE p in State 1: 0.94, 0.96
- Varying BLACK p in State 1: 0.05 seems optimal, try 0.06, 0.07

**Exploration:**
Since we're stuck at -17.05, try genuinely different approaches:
- Try GoToColor CYAN (6) instead of BLUE - maybe robots can perceive cyan walls?
- Try different LED colors (shouldn't matter functionally)
- Try swapping: GoToColor RED as first state, GoToColor BLUE as second
- Try adding GRAY floor trigger to State 0 with very small probability

Actually, the 3rd transition consistently hurts. Stay with 2-transition states.

Let me also try: What if we use p=0.9/0.05 in State 0 and vary State 1 more carefully around 0.95/0.05?

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Situation assessment:**
The champion (-17.05) hasn't improved for 3 rounds despite extensive fine-tuning. We've thoroughly explored the parameter space around the 2-state GoToColor BLUE → GoToColor RED structure. The results show:

- p=0.9 for BLACK in State 0 is optimal
- p=0.05 for WHITE in State 0 is optimal  
- p=0.95-0.96 for WHITE in State 1 works best
- p=0.05-0.08 for BLACK in State 1 works best
- 3rd transitions consistently hurt
- 3-state machines hurt

**We're likely at a local optimum.** Time for genuine exploration to escape it.

**New ideas to try:**
1. The scoring: BLUE side decreases score (good), GREEN side increases score (bad). What if there's a way to be MORE selective? Maybe robots can perceive BLUE/GREEN walls directly with camera condition C=7?
2. Try using color perception (C=7, l=3 for BLUE) to trigger transitions - detect when near blue walls
3. Try GoToColor CYAN (6) - maybe there's something cyan in the arena?
4. Try 1-state GoToColor BLUE only (simpler than champion, no return mode confusion)
5. Try using GRAY floor trigger: when crossing gray (middle of arena) switch states
6. Try a 3-state machine where state 2 handles recovery via exploration
7. Try GoAwayColor GREEN + GoToColor RED combination (avoid bad source, find nest)
8. Try different LED colors to test if LED matters
9. Try swapping: State 0 = GoToColor RED, State 1 = GoToColor BLUE with inverted floor triggers

**Key insight I haven't tried:** What if robots should switch state when they SEE blue (camera C=7, l=3 BLUE) rather than when on black floor? That would be more reliable.

Also: The champion transitions from State 0→State 1 on BLACK (got item) AND on WHITE (p=0.05, at nest). What if p=0.05 is slightly wrong - what about 0.02 or 0.01?

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key finding:** The new champion uses p=0.01 for the WHITE floor trigger in State 0 (down from 0.05). This makes sense: robots in "go to source" mode should almost never switch to "go to nest" mode just because they're on white floor - they might be passing through. A very low probability (0.01) is better than 0.05.

**What we've learned:**
- State 0 (GoToColor BLUE): BLACK p=0.9 → S1 (got item), WHITE p=0.01 → S1 (almost never escape nest accidentally)
- State 1 (GoToColor RED): WHITE p=0.95 → S0 (delivered), BLACK p=0.05 → S0 (recovery)
- GRAY triggers HURT (worst performer -3.78 from round 5)
- Camera-based triggers in State 0 HURT (-2.73)
- GRAY floor trigger in State 1 HURT (8.68, 10.20 with GRAY)
- 3-state variant scored -16.51 (decent but not better than champion)

**Exploitation:**
- Try p=0.005, 0.008, 0.012, 0.015, 0.02 for WHITE trigger in State 0
- Try p=0.0 (remove it entirely) - but wait, we need at least 1 transition, and if we use 1 transition it drops the second
- Try varying BLACK trigger in State 0: 0.88, 0.91, 0.92, 0.93
- Try varying State 1 parameters: WHITE p=0.94, 0.96, 0.97; BLACK p=0.04, 0.06
- Try p=0.01 WHITE in State 0 combined with different State 1 params

**Exploration:**
- Try 1-state GoToColor BLUE only (no return - robots might naturally oscillate)
- Try 3-state variant from rank 3 with parameter tuning
- Try the champion with slightly different combinations

**Important:** GRAY trigger (c=1) in State 1 is catastrophic. Avoid it completely.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 21 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.65 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
